Figure 5
.
Selected syntax and semantics of constraints.

To reason about constraint-based inference, we need more than a procedure for
generating and solving constraints: we require a
formal logic
of
constraints, with a syntax and a declarative semantics that characterizes
satisfiability. This semantics is essential: it validates the design of our
constraint language and provides the foundation for proving the soundness,
completeness, and principality of inference. Without it, the meta-theory of our
approach cannot be stated precisely.

We now introduce the syntax and semantics of our constraint language.
Building atop the constraint-based inference framework of

29
, we adopt a constraint language
(
Figure

5
) that includes both term and type variables. Its
semantics is given by a satisfiability judgment
\phi\vdash C

(
Figure

5
). The semantic environment
\phi
assigns to each
free type variable
\alpha
a ground type
{\mathfrak{g}}\in\mathcal{G}
(a type with no
free variables) and to each term variable
x
a set of ground
types
10
10

10

\mathfrak{S}
can be pronounced “Fraktur S” or “ground S”.

\mathfrak{S}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
(the instances of a type scheme bound to

x
).
We write
\phi[\alpha:={\mathfrak{g}}]
and
\phi[x:=\mathfrak{S}]
for
extensions of
\phi
, and
\phi(\tau)
for the ground type obtained by
substitution.

Constraints include basic logical forms: tautological
\mathsf{true}
(
True
),
unsatisfiable
\mathsf{false}
, and conjunctive
{C_{1}}\wedge{C_{2}}
(
Conj
)
constraints. The unification constraint
{{\tau_{1}}}={{\tau_{2}}}
is satisfied when

{\tau_{1}}
and
{\tau_{2}}
are equal (
Unif
).
An existential constraint
\exists{\alpha}.\,C
holds if there exists a witness

{\mathfrak{g}}
for
\alpha
satisfying
C
(
Exists
), while a universal constraint

\forall{\alpha}.\,C
holds if
C
is satisfied for every binding of
\alpha

(
Forall
).
When
\sigma
is a polymorphic type scheme
\forall{{\bar{\alpha}}}.\,{{\tau^{\prime}}}
, we use the notation

\sigma\leq\tau
as shorthand for the instantiation constraint
\exists{{\bar{\alpha}}}.\,{{\tau^{\prime}}}={\tau}
.
OmniML
-specific constraints, such as record label
instantiation
t.\ell\leq{\tau_{1}}\to{\tau_{2}}

(
Section

2.4
), are introduced later (
Section

4.2
).

Polymorphism in the constraint language is expressed through

generalization
and
instantiation
constraints. In a generalization
constraint (or
let
-constraint)
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x=\lambda{\alpha}.\,{{C_{1}}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{2}}
, the definition of

x
is a constraint abstraction
\lambda{\alpha}.\,{{C_{1}}}
, a function that, when applied
to a type
\tau
, returns
{C_{1}}[\alpha:=\tau]
. The binding of
x
is then
available in
{C_{2}}
and refers to this abstraction. Semantically, the
abstraction
\lambda{\alpha}.\,{{C_{1}}}
is interpreted as a set of ground types that
satisfies
{C_{1}}
, and the generalization constraint requires this set to be
non-empty,
i.e.
, there is at least one instantiation of
\alpha
that satisfies

{C_{1}}
.
Instantiations (or applications)
x\;\tau
eliminate abstractions by
applying a type
\tau
to the abstraction bound to
x
. This holds precisely
when
\phi(\tau)\in\phi(x)
,
i.e.
,
\tau
is one of the satisfiable
instances of
x
. The
\lambda
-abstraction and application syntax follows

31
. In other presentations, constraint
abstractions
\lambda{\alpha}.\,{\exists{{\bar{\beta}}}.\,C}
are written as constrained type
schemes
\forall\alpha,{\bar{\beta}}.\,C\Rightarrow\alpha
, and instantiation constraints

x\;\tau
are written
x\leq\tau
.

Finally, we introduce
suspended match constraints

(\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\tau\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}{\bar{\chi}})
,
which consist of:

(1)

A matchee
\tau
. The constraint remains suspended
until the
shape
of
\tau
is determined,
i.e.
, while
\tau
is a type variable.

(2)

A list of branches
{\bar{\chi}}
of the form
\rho\Rightarrow C
, where

\rho
is a shape pattern.
11
11

11

The match constraints in this
paper only use a single branch; but in a larger language there
would be use-cases for having several branches; for example,
record projection could be overloaded to work both on nominal
records and on tuples (or objects, modules, etc.), requiring
several branches in its generated constraint. So we kept the
general syntax.
The constraint
C
is solved in the extended
context produced by the matching pattern.

For example, the wildcard pattern
\mathord{\_}
matches any shape,
and binds nothing.
To ensure determinism, the set of patterns
\bar{\rho}
must be

disjoint
—that is, no shape may be matched by more than one pattern
in the list.

The formal semantics of suspended match constraints are somewhat involved;
we return to them in the next subsection (
Section

4.1
).

\frac{\frac{\frac{}{\mathsf{int}=\mathsf{int}}}{\phi[\alpha:=\mathsf{int}]\vdash{\alpha}={\mathsf{int}}}\quad\textsc{Right=Unif}}{\phi\vdash\exists{\alpha}.\,{\alpha}={\mathsf{int}}}\quad\textsc{right=Exists}

Closed constraints are either satisfiable in any semantic environment (
i.e.
, they are tautologies) or unsatisfiable. For example, the satisfiability of
the constraint
\exists{\alpha}.\,{\alpha}={\mathsf{int}}
is established by the
derivation on the right-hand side.

We write
{C_{1}}\vDash{C_{2}}
to express that
{C_{1}}

entails

{C_{2}}
,
meaning every solution
\phi
to
{C_{1}}
is also a solution to
{C_{2}}
.
We write
{C_{1}}\equiv{C_{2}}
to indicate that
{C_{1}}
and
{C_{2}}
are equivalent,
that is, they have exactly the same set of solutions.

Throughout this paper, we will find it convenient to work with
constraint
contexts
. A constraint context
\mathscr{C}
is simply a constraint with a
hole
,
analogous to term contexts
\mathscr{E}
introduced in
Section

3
. We write

\mathscr{C}[C]
to denote filling the hole of the context
\mathscr{C}
with the
constraint
C
. Hole filling may capture variables, so we frequently require
explicit side conditions when variable capture must be avoided. We write
\mathsf{bv}({\mathscr{C}})
for the set of variables bound at the hole in
\mathscr{C}
.

4.1.
Suspended constraints

A central difficulty in our work on suspended constraints was defining a
satisfying semantics. The challenge lies in formalizing what it means for type
information to be
known
without presupposing a
static
solving
order. This is the same issue encountered in our typing rules
(
Section

3.4
), and we address it in the same way: by introducing a
contextual rule together with a unicity condition.

To define the semantics for suspended constraints, we first introduce

discharged match constraints
.

Definition 4.1 (Discharged match constraint).

Given a suspended constraint
(\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\tau\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}{\bar{\chi}})
and a canonical shape

\varsigma
, we introduce the syntactic sugar
(\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\tau:=\varsigma\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}{\bar{\chi}})
for the

discharged match constraint
that selects the branch in
{\bar{\chi}}
that
matches
\varsigma
:

\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\tau:=\varsigma\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}\overline{\rho\Rightarrow C}\hskip 4.30554pt\mathrel{\triangleq}\hskip 4.30554pt\begin{cases}\exists{{\bar{\alpha}}}.\,{\tau}={\varsigma\,}{\bar{\alpha}}\wedge\theta({C_{i}})&\text{if }{\rho_{i}}\mathrel{\mathsf{matches}}\varsigma\;{\bar{\alpha}}\hookrightarrow\theta\\
\mathsf{false}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

The first conjunct (
\tau=\varsigma\,{\bar{\alpha}}
) ensures that
\varsigma
is indeed the
canonical shape of
\tau
, and the second conjunct is the selected branch
constraint
{C_{i}}
under the appropriate substitution. Since the syntax of
suspended match constraints requires that branch patterns are
non-overlapping, the matching branch
{\rho_{i}}\Rightarrow{C_{i}}
is uniquely
determined. It may, however, be undefined if the branches are not exhaustive,
in which case the discharged constraint is
\mathsf{false}
.

The partial function
(\rho\mathrel{\mathsf{matches}}\varsigma\;{\bar{\alpha}})
, introduced in

Figure

5
, describes how a pattern
\rho
is matched against a
canonical principal shape
\varsigma
. Before matching,
\varsigma
is applied with fresh
shape variables
{\bar{\alpha}}
(of the same arity as
\varsigma
), so that the result of
matching may refer to them. The match either fails or returns a substitution

\theta
mapping pattern variables (
e.g.
record name variables
t
from

Section

2.4
) to shape components (
e.g.
record names
T
). These
components may themselves mention the freshly introduced variables
{\bar{\alpha}}
.
In
Figure

5
, we only introduce the wildcard pattern and its
matching rule; in
Section

4.2
we extend both the syntax of
shape patterns and the matching function itself to handle the patterns that
arise in
OmniML
.

A natural attempt

As with typing rules for fragile constructs (
e.g.

Rcd-Proj-I-Nat
), suspended
match constraints have a
natural rule
:

\frac{\varsigma=\mathsf{shape}(\phi(\tau))\quad\phi\vdash\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\tau:=\varsigma\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}{\bar{\chi}}}{\phi\vdash\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\tau\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}{\bar{\chi}}}\quad\textsc{Match-Nat}

This rule states that a suspended constraint holds whenever the corresponding
discharged constraint holds for the canonical shape of
\phi(\tau)
.

Although simple and declarative, this semantics is too
permissive. It allows
guessing
the shape of
\tau
rather than requiring
it to be
known
. For example,
\exists{\alpha}.\,\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\alpha\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}\mathord{\_}\Rightarrow{\alpha}={\mathsf{int}}
is satisfiable under the natural semantics:

\frac{\mathord{\_}\mathrel{\mathsf{matches}}\mathsf{int}\;\emptyset\hookrightarrow\emptyset\quad\frac{\mathsf{int}=\mathsf{int}}{\phi[\alpha:=\mathsf{int}]\vdash{\alpha}={\mathsf{int}}}\quad\textsc{Right=Unif}}{\frac{\phi[\alpha:=\mathsf{int}]\vdash\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\alpha\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}\mathord{\_}\Rightarrow{\alpha}={\mathsf{int}}}{\phi\vdash\exists{\alpha}.\,\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\alpha\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}\mathord{\_}\Rightarrow{\alpha}={\mathsf{int}}}\quad\textsc{Right=Exists}}\quad\textsc{Right=Match-Nat}

This “out-of-thin-air” behavior does not match the intended
meaning of suspended match constraints and raises several problems:

(1)

a reasonable solver—one that avoids backtracking—cannot
be complete with respect to this semantics; and

(2)

it breaks the existence of principal solutions, just
as the
natural
typing rules do (
e.g.

Rcd-Proj-I-Nat
).

Contextual semantics

To rule out guessing, we instead adopt a
contextual
semantics: a
match constraint is satisfiable only if the shape of the type is determined
by the surrounding context. The corresponding rule for suspended
constraints,
Match-Ctx
(
Figure

5
), is the only non-syntax-directed rule in our semantics.

\frac{\mathscr{C}[\tau\mathop{!}\varsigma]\quad\phi\vdash\mathscr{C}[\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\tau:=\varsigma\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}{\bar{\chi}}]}{\phi\vdash\mathscr{C}[\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\tau\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}{\bar{\chi}}]}\quad\textsc{Match-Ctx}

In this rule, a suspended constraint
(\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\tau\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}{\bar{\chi}})
in the context

\mathscr{C}
can be discharged, provided the shape
\varsigma
is not guessed from
\phi
,
but recovered from the constraint context
\mathscr{C}
. This
unicity
condition

\mathscr{C}[\tau\mathop{!}\varsigma]
(defined below) ensures that
\varsigma
is uniquely determined
by the context
\mathscr{C}
, capturing precisely what it means for the shape of a type
to be
known
.

Definition 4.2 (Erasure).

The erasure
\lfloor C\rfloor
of a constraint
C
is defined as the constraint
obtained by replacing suspended match constraints in
C
with
\mathsf{true}
.

Definition 4.3 (Simple constraints).

We say that
C
is
simple
if it contains no suspended match
constraints.

Definition 4.4 (Unicity).

We define the unicity condition
\mathscr{C}[\tau\mathop{!}\varsigma]
, which states that
\tau

has a unique canonical shape
\varsigma
within the context
\mathscr{C}
as:

\forall\phi,{\mathfrak{g}}.\hskip 4.30554pt\phi\vdash\lfloor\mathscr{C}[{\tau}={{\mathfrak{g}}}]\rfloor\implies\mathsf{shape}({\mathfrak{g}})=\varsigma

.

Similarly to the unicity conditions introduced for typing rules in

Section

3.4
, unicity
for constraints
\mathscr{C}[\tau\mathop{!}\varsigma]
relies on
erasure

\lfloor\mathscr{C}[{\tau}={{\mathfrak{g}}}]\rfloor
to restrict attention to previously discharged
match constraints, inducing a causal order between match constraints,
enforced by
Match-Ctx
.

When
\tau
is not a variable,
\square[\tau\mathop{!}\varsigma]
holds trivially when

\varsigma
is the shape of
\tau
. Likewise, when (the erasure of)
\mathscr{C}
is
unsatisfiable, then
\mathscr{C}[\alpha\mathop{!}\varsigma]
holds vacuously for any
\varsigma
. The
interesting and nontrivial cases—the ones we illustrate next—arise when

\tau
is a type variable and
\mathscr{C}
is satisfiable.

Example 4.5.

Recall the problematic example that was satisfiable under the natural
semantics:

\exists{\alpha}.\,\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\alpha\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}\mathord{\_}\Rightarrow{\alpha}={\mathsf{int}}

Under the contextual semantics, the suspended constraint appears in a
context
\mathscr{C}

with no contextual information.
i.e.
,
\mathscr{C}
is
\exists{\alpha}.\,\square
.
So for any ground type
{\mathfrak{g}}
,
\lfloor\mathscr{C}[{\alpha}={{\mathfrak{g}}}]\rfloor
(
i.e.
,
\exists{\alpha}.\,{\alpha}={{\mathfrak{g}}}
) is satisfiable, allowing
{\mathfrak{g}}
to have an
arbitrary shape (
e.g.

\mathsf{int}
,
\mathsf{bool}
,
etc.
). As a result, the uniqueness
condition
\mathscr{C}[\alpha\mathop{!}\varsigma]
never holds making
Match-Ctx

inapplicable. The constraint is unsatisfiable as intended.

Example 4.6.

Consider the satisfiable constraint:

\exists{\alpha}.\,{\alpha}={\mathsf{int}}\wedge\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\alpha\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}\mathord{\_}\Rightarrow\mathsf{true}

Here, we apply the contextual rule with the context
\mathscr{C}
equal to
\exists{\alpha}.\,{\alpha}={\mathsf{int}}\wedge\square
. Any solution
\phi
of this context
necessarily satisfies
{\alpha}={\mathsf{int}}
, so we have
\mathscr{C}[\alpha\mathop{!}\mathsf{int}]
and the suspended constraint can be discharged.

Example 4.7.

Consider the more intricate example:

\exists{\alpha,\beta}.\,(\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\alpha\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}\mathord{\_}\Rightarrow{\beta}={\mathsf{bool}})\;\wedge\;(\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\beta\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}\mathord{\_}\Rightarrow\mathsf{true})\;\wedge\;({\alpha}={\mathsf{int}})

Suppose we attempt to apply
Match-Ctx
to the match on
\beta
first. We
want to show
\mathscr{C}[\beta\mathop{!}\mathsf{bool}]
for the context
\mathscr{C}
equal to

(\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\alpha\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}\mathord{\_}\Rightarrow{\beta}={\mathsf{bool}})\wedge\square\wedge{\alpha}={\mathsf{int}}
. Its erasure
\lfloor\mathscr{C}\rfloor
is
\mathsf{true}\wedge\square\wedge{\alpha}={\mathsf{int}}
, which imposes no constraints on
\beta
. Thus both
\lfloor{\mathscr{C}[{\beta}={\mathsf{int}}]}\rfloor
and
\lfloor\mathscr{C}[{\beta}={\mathsf{bool}}]\rfloor
are both satisfiable: unicity does not hold and
Match-Ctx

cannot be applied.

By contrast, if we first discharge the match on
\alpha
, we consider the
context
\mathscr{C}
equal to
\square\wedge(\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\beta\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}\mathord{\_}\Rightarrow\mathsf{true})\wedge{\alpha}={\mathsf{int}}
. Its erasure
\lfloor\mathscr{C}\rfloor
equal to
\square\wedge\mathsf{true}\wedge{\alpha}={\mathsf{int}}
does constraint
\alpha
, giving
\mathscr{C}[\alpha\mathop{!}\mathsf{int}]
.
We may therefore discharge the match on
\alpha
, rewriting it as

(\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\alpha:=\mathsf{int}\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}\mathord{\_}\Rightarrow{\beta}={\mathsf{bool}})

i.e.
,
\alpha=\mathsf{int}\wedge{\beta}={\mathsf{bool}}
. Substituting back, we
are left to satisfy the constraint
\mathscr{C}[\alpha=\mathsf{int}\wedge{\beta}={\mathsf{bool}}]

i.e.
,

{\alpha}={\mathsf{int}}\wedge{\beta}={\mathsf{bool}}\wedge(\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\beta\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}\mathord{\_}\Rightarrow\mathsf{true})\wedge{\alpha}={\mathsf{int}}

.
At this point, unicity for
\beta
holds, since the context now includes
{\beta}={\mathsf{bool}}
. We can therefore apply
Match-Ctx
to eliminate the final
match constraint.

This example demonstrates that suspended constraints must be resolved in
a dependency-respecting order: attempting to resolve a match constraint too
early may result in unsatisfiability.

Example 4.8.

Let us consider a constraint with a cyclic dependency between match
constraints:

\exists{\alpha,\beta}.\,(\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\alpha\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}\mathord{\_}\Rightarrow{\beta}={\mathsf{bool}})\;\wedge\;(\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\beta\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}\mathord{\_}\Rightarrow{\alpha}={\mathsf{int}})

Under the natural semantics this constraint is satisfiable, since one may

guess
the assignment
\alpha:=\mathsf{int},\beta:=\mathsf{bool}
,
making both match constraints succeed. However, our solver and contextual semantics
reject it.

Without loss of generality, suppose we attempt to apply
Match-Ctx
on

\alpha
first. We must establish
\mathscr{C}[\alpha\mathop{!}\mathsf{int}]
for the context
\mathscr{C}:=\square\wedge\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\beta\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}\mathord{\_}\Rightarrow{\alpha}={\mathsf{int}}
. But
the erasure
\lfloor\mathscr{C}\rfloor
is
\square\wedge\mathsf{true}
, which imposes no constraint on

\alpha
. Hence unicity fails and
Match-Ctx
is inapplicable.

4.2.

{}\mathrel{\triangleq}{}

{}\exists{{\bar{\alpha}}}.\,\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\llbracket{e_{i}}:{\alpha_{i}}\rrbracket{}

{}\llbracket e:\forall{{\bar{\alpha}}}.\,{\tau}\rrbracket{}

{}\mathrel{\triangleq}{}

{}\forall{{\bar{\alpha}}}.\,\llbracket e:\tau\rrbracket{}

Figure 7
.
The constraint generation translation for
OmniML
.

We now present the formal translation from terms
e
to constraints
C
,
such that the resulting constraint is satisfiable if and only if the term is
well typed. The translation is defined as a function
\llbracket e:\tau\rrbracket
, where
e

is the term to be translated and
\tau
is the expected type of
e
.
The expected type
\tau
is permitted to contain type variables, which can be
existentially bound in order to perform type inference. The models of
constraint
\llbracket e:\tau\rrbracket
interpret the free variables of

\tau
such that
\tau
becomes a valid type of
e
. For example, to infer the
entire type of
e
we may pick a fresh type variable
\alpha
for
\tau
.

Shape patterns

Thus far, our formal presentation of shape patterns has remained
abstract, deliberately leaving the syntax and semantics of match constraints
partially unspecified to accommodate a range of language features. We now
concretize this by specifying the shape patterns used in
OmniML
(see
Figure

6
), and introducing the corresponding constraints
for the variables they bind.
Shape patterns include:

(1)

Record type patterns
\mathsf{rcd}\;{t}\;{\mathord{\_}}
, binding the name
T

of a record type
\mathsf{rcd}\;{T}\;{{\bar{\tau}}}
to the record variable
t
.

(2)

Polytype patterns
[s]
matching a polytype
[\sigma]
and
binding the resulting scheme to the variable
s
.

Each new kind of pattern introduces corresponding constraint formers:

t.\ell\leq{\tau_{1}}\to{\tau_{2}}
asserts that
{\tau_{1}}\to{\tau_{2}}
is a
instance of projection type associated with the explicit label
t.\ell
;
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{dom}}t={\bar{\ell}}
ensures that the domain of record type

t
is the set of labels
{\bar{\ell}}
; and
s\leq\tau
checks that
\tau
is
an instance of
s
, while
x\leq s
asserts that every instance of

x
is an instance of
s
.

By definition, each of these constraint forms is unsatisfiable, since we do not
extend the satisfiability relation to include them. Their role is purely
syntactic: they only appear within the branches of a suspended match
constraint. Once such a constraint is discharged
(\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\tau:=\varsigma\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}{\bar{\rho}}\Rightarrow{\bar{C}})
(
Definition

4.1
), the substitution
\theta
produced by
the matching pattern
{\rho_{i}}
—which binds the pattern variables (
e.g.

t
,
s
,
etc.
)—is
applied to the corresponding branch
{C_{i}}
. Hence, it suffices to define
the semantics of these constraint formers only for their substituted forms (
e.g.

T.\ell\leq{\tau_{1}}\to{\tau_{2}}
,
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{dom}}{T}={\bar{\ell}}
,
etc.
), as shown in

Figure

6
. We define the semantics of these substituted forms by
translation into existing constraints—that is, as syntactic sugar over the
existing core constraint language.

Constraint generation

Constraint generation
\llbracket e:\mathop{\tau}\rrbracket
is defined in

Figure

7
. All generated type variables are fresh with respect
to the expected type
\tau
, ensuring capture-avoidance. We now review the cases
of the constraint generator, beginning with the cases corresponding to
traditional
ML
constructs.

Unsurprisingly, variables
x
generate an instantiation constraint
x\;\tau
. The unit term
()
requires
\tau
to be the unit type
1
. A
function generates a constraint that binds two fresh flexible type variables
for the parameter and return types. We use a let-constraint to bind the
parameter in the constraint generated for the body of the function. The
let-constraint is monomorphic since
{\alpha^{\prime}}
is fully constrained by type
variables defined outside the abstraction’s scope and therefore cannot be
generalized. Applications introduce two fresh flexible type variables, one for
the argument type and one for the type of the function, typing each subterm
with these, ensuring
\tau
is the expected return type. Let-bindings generate a
polymorphic let-constraint;
\lambda{\alpha}.\,{\llbracket e:\alpha\rrbracket}
is a principal
constraint abstraction for
e
: its intended interpretation is the set of all
types that
e
admits. Annotations bind their flexible type variables and
enforce the equality of the annotated type
{\tau^{\prime}}
and the expected type
\tau
.

For polytypes, explicit boxing asserts that
e
has the polymorphic type
\sigma

(using universal quantification) and that the expected type is the polytype

[\sigma]
. Conversely, explicit unboxing requires that
\tau
be an instance
of
\sigma
. These cases introduce no suspended match constrains because the
annotations can the required type information: the polytype
[\sigma]
.

By contrast, implicit unboxing suspends until the inferred type of
e
is
known to be some non-variable type
{\tau_{0}}
. At that point, the suspended match
constraint attempts to match
{\tau_{0}}
against the pattern
[s]
. If

{\tau_{0}}=[\sigma]
, the match succeeds, binding
\sigma
to
s
and requiring

\tau
to be an instance of
s
(
i.e.
,
\sigma\leq\tau
); otherwise, the match
fails and the term is ill-typed.

Implicit boxing behaves dually: we infer the principal type for
e
using a
let-constraint and suspend until the expected type of the entire term
resolves to some type
{\tau_{0}}
. If
{\tau_{0}}
matches the same pattern
[s]
(
i.e.
,
{\tau_{0}}=[\sigma]
), we assert that the principal type of
e
is
at least as general as
s
, via the constraint
x\leq s
(
i.e.
,
x\leq\sigma
); otherwise, the match fails.

Record constructs are handled in a similar way, but their matching patterns
concern record shapes rather than polytypes. A record projection generate a
fresh variable
\alpha
for the record type and constrain
e
to this type,
suspending until the type of
e
(
\alpha
) resolves to some type
{\tau_{0}}
. When

{\tau_{0}}=\mathsf{rcd}\;{T}\;{{\bar{\tau}}}
, the shape pattern
(\mathsf{rcd}\;{t}\;{\mathord{\_}})
matches successfully,
binding
T
to the record name variable
t
; the matching branch then
retrieves the projected label’s type from the global label context
\Omega

and instantiates it to match
\alpha\to\tau
. If
{\tau_{0}}
is not a
record type, the match fails and the term is ill-typed.

For record expressions, we generate a fresh variable
{\alpha_{i}}
for each field
assignment to capture the type of each
{e_{i}}
as
{\alpha_{i}}
. The rest of the
constraint is deferred until the context determines the type of the whole
record type to be
{\tau_{0}}
. Once known, it is matched against the same pattern

(\mathsf{rcd}\;{t}\;{\mathord{\_}})
. If the match succeeds (
i.e.
,
{\tau_{0}}=\mathsf{rcd}\;{T}\;{{\bar{\tau}}}
and

t=T
) the labels are instantiated to match the projection types
\tau\to{\alpha_{i}}
, and we additionally check that the domain of
t
is exactly
{\bar{\ell}}
,
ensuring that every label is defined. Otherwise, the constraint is
unsatisfiable as intended.

Explicit records and projections, along with closed-world disambiguated terms,
bypass suspension and directly instantiate the appropriate labels.

Example 4.9.

Considering the example from
Section

2.1
:

⬇

let

ex_4

r

=

let

x

=

r
.
x

in

x

+

(
r

:

point
).
y

°\
ocamlflags

10°

The typing constraint generated for
ex_4
contains the following,
where
\alpha
stands for the type of
r
:

\exists{\alpha}.\,\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x=\lambda{\beta}.\,{(\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\alpha\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}\dots)}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}x\;\mathsf{int}\wedge{\alpha}={\texttt{point}}

The suspended constraint can be discharged under our contextual semantics.
We apply the
Match-Ctx
rule with context

\mathscr{C}
equal to

\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x=\lambda{\beta}.\,{\square}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}x\;\mathsf{int}\wedge{\alpha}={\texttt{point}}

.
Although the context includes a let-binding—which in practice
involves let-generalization—we can still deduce
\mathscr{C}[\alpha\mathop{!}\texttt{point}]
, since the erased context
\lfloor\mathscr{C}\rfloor
contains the unification constraint
{\alpha}={\texttt{point}}
.

This example illustrates that our formulation of suspended constraints
interacts nicely with let-polymorphism. Although the two features are
specified in a modular fashion, they are carefully crafted to work together,
as we further show in our next example.

Example 4.10.

A subtle yet crucial feature of our semantics is its support for

backpropagation
:

⬇

let

ex_1_1

=

let

getx

r

=

r
.
x

in

getx

one

°\
ocamlflags

10°

As in the previous example, the type of
r
cannot be disambiguated from
the let-definition alone. There, the ambiguity was resolved when the type
(
\alpha
) was unified to a known type (
point
) in the let-body. Here, the
situation is more subtle: an
instance
of
getx
’s type scheme is
taken, which only satisfies the application (
getx

one
) if
r

has either a variable type or the record type
point
. However, the
projection
r
.
x
would be ill-typed if
r
had a variable type
(unicity would fail), so
point
is the unique consistent solution. This
information therefore flows back from the instances in the let-body to the
let-definition, a phenomenon we call
backpropagation
.

The constraint generated when typing

ex_1_1
is:

{}\exists{\alpha}.{}

{}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}getx=\lambda{\delta}.\,{\exists{\beta,\gamma}.\,\left({\delta}={\beta\to\gamma}\wedge\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\beta\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}\dots\right)}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}getx\;(\texttt{point}\to\alpha){}

With the context
\mathscr{C}
equal to
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}getx=\lambda{\delta}.\,{\exists{\beta,\gamma}.\,{\delta}={\beta\to\gamma}\wedge\square}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}getx\;(\texttt{point}\to\alpha)
, we can show that the unicity
predicate
\mathscr{C}[\beta\mathop{!}\texttt{point}]
holds.
For any
{\mathfrak{g}}
, the erasure
\lfloor\mathscr{C}[{\beta}={{\mathfrak{g}}}]\rfloor

is

\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}getx=\lambda{\delta}.\,{\exists{\beta,\gamma}.\,{\delta}={\beta\to\gamma}\wedge{\beta}={{\mathfrak{g}}}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}getx\;(\texttt{point}\to\alpha)

.
Since
getx
is bound to the constraint abstraction

\lambda{\delta}.\,{\exists}\gamma.\hskip 4.30554pt{\delta}={({\mathfrak{g}}\to\gamma)}
,
the instantiation

getx\;(\texttt{point}\to\alpha)

can only be satisfied when
{\mathfrak{g}}
is equal to
point
. This proves unicity,
hence the generated constraint for
ex_1_1
is satisfiable.

4.3.
Metatheory

Constraint generation is sound and complete with respect to the typing judgment:

Theorem 4.11 (Constraint generation is sound and complete).

A closed
OmniML
term
e
is typable if and only if
the constraint
\exists{\alpha}.\,\llbracket e:\alpha\rrbracket
is satisfiable.

Theorem 4.12 (Principal types).

For any well-typed closed
OmniML
term
e
, there exists a type
\tau

such that:

(1)

\vdash e:\tau
.

(2)

For any other typing
\vdash e:{\tau^{\prime}}
, then
{\tau^{\prime}}=\theta(\tau)
for some
substitution
\theta
.

5.
Constraint solving

We now present a machine for solving constraints in our language. The solver
operates as a rewriting system on constraints
C\longrightarrow{C^{\prime}}
. Once no
further transitions are applicable,
i.e.
,
C\longarrownot\longrightarrow
, the constraint
C

is either a solved form—from which we can read off a most general
solution—or unsatisfiable (if the constraint

C
is closed).

5.1.
Unification

{}U{}

{}::={}

{}\mathsf{true}\mid\mathsf{false}\mid{U_{1}}\wedge{U_{2}}\mid\exists{\alpha}.\,U\mid\epsilon{}

Unification problems

{}\epsilon{}

{}::={}

{}\emptyset\mid{\tau}={\epsilon}{}

Multi-equations

{}C{}

{}::={}

{}\dots\mid\epsilon{}

Constraints

{}\mathscr{U}{}

{}::={}

{}\square\mid\mathscr{U}\wedge{U_{2}}\mid{U_{1}}\wedge\mathscr{U}\mid\exists{\alpha}.\,\mathscr{U}{}

Unification context

\frac{\forall\tau\in\epsilon.\,\,\phi(\tau)={\mathfrak{g}}}{\phi\vdash\epsilon}

Multi-Unif

(a)

Syntax and semantics of unification problems.

\frac{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\exists{\alpha}.\,{U_{1}})\wedge{U_{2}}\quad\alpha\mathop{\#}{U_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\exists{\alpha}.\,{U_{1}}\wedge{U_{2}}}\quad\textsc{U-Exists}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U\quad\mathsf{cyclic}(U)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{false}}\quad\textsc{U-Cycle}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U\wedge\mathsf{true}\quad}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U}\quad\textsc{U-True}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathscr{U}[\mathsf{false}]\quad\mathscr{U}\neq\square}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{false}}\quad\textsc{U-False}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\alpha}={{\epsilon_{1}}}\wedge{\alpha}={{\epsilon_{2}}}\quad}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\alpha}={{{\epsilon_{1}}}={{\epsilon_{2}}}}}\quad\textsc{U-Merge}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\alpha}={{\alpha}={\epsilon}}\quad}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\alpha}={\epsilon}}\quad\textsc{U-Stutter}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\varsigma\,({\bar{\tau}},{\tau_{i}},{\bar{\tau}^{\prime}})}={\epsilon}\quad\alpha\mathop{\#}{\bar{\tau}},{\bar{\tau}^{\prime}},\epsilon\quad{\tau_{i}}\notin\mathcal{V}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\exists{\alpha}.\,{\alpha}={{\tau_{i}}}\wedge{\varsigma\,({\bar{\tau}},\alpha,{\bar{\tau}^{\prime}})}={\epsilon}}\quad\textsc{U-Name}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\varsigma\,{\bar{\alpha}}}={{\varsigma\,{\bar{\beta}}}={\epsilon}}\quad}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\varsigma\,{\bar{\alpha}}}={\epsilon}\wedge{{\bar{\alpha}}}={{\bar{\beta}}}}\quad\textsc{U-Decomp}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\varsigma\,{\bar{\alpha}}}={{{\varsigma^{\prime}}\,{\bar{\beta}}}={\epsilon}}\quad\varsigma\neq{\varsigma^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{false}}\quad\textsc{U-Clash}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon\quad|\epsilon|\leq 1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{true}}\quad\textsc{U-Trivial}

(b)

Unification algorithm as a series of rewriting rules

{U_{1}}\longrightarrow{U_{2}}
. All shapes are principal.

Figure 8
.
Unification: syntax, semantics, and the rewriting-based solver
{U_{1}}\longrightarrow{U_{2}}
.

Our constraints ultimately reduce to equations between types, which we solve
using first-order unification. Like our solver, we specify unification as a
non-deterministic rewriting relation between
unification problems

{U_{1}}\longrightarrow{U_{2}}
, that eventually reduces to a solved form
\hat{U}
or to
\mathsf{false}
.

Unification problems
U

(
Figure

8(a)
) are a restricted subset
of constraints, extended with
multi-equations

(
29
)
—a multi-set of types considered
equal. These generalize binary equalities:
\phi
satisfies
a multi-equation
\epsilon
if all of its members are mapped to a single
ground type
{\mathfrak{g}}
(
Multi-Unif
). Multi-equations are
considered equal modulo permutation of their members.

The unification rules are listed in
Figure

8(b)
. Rewriting
proceeds under an arbitrary context
\mathscr{U}
, modulo
\alpha
-equivalence and
associativity and commutativity of conjunctions.
Our algorithm is largely standard
(
29
)
, with its main
novelty being the use of
canonical principal shapes
in place of type
constructors. This uniform treatment of monotypes and polytypes simplifies
unification and improves on the previous treatment of polytype unification

(
6
)
.

We briefly summarize the role of each rule.
U-Exists
lifts existential
quantifiers, enabling applications of
U-Merge
and
U-Cycle
since
all multi-equations eventually become part of a single conjunction.

U-Merge
combines multi-equations sharing a common variable and

U-Stutter
removes duplicate variables.
U-Decomp
decomposes equal
types with matching shapes into equalities between their subcomponents, while

U-Clash
detects shape mismatches that result in failure.
U-Name

introduces fresh variables for subcomponents, ensuring unification operates on

shallow terms
, making sharing of type variables explicit and avoiding
copying types in rules such as
U-Decomp
.
U-True
and

U-Trivial
eliminate trivial constraints, and
U-False
propagates
failure.
Finally,
U-Cycle
implements the
occurs check
, ensuring that a
type variable does not occur in the type it is being unified with. This is a
necessary condition for unification, as it would otherwise lead to infinite
types.
This is formalized by the relation
\alpha\prec_{U}\beta
indicating that

\alpha
occurs in a type equated to
\beta
in
U
, that is, when
U=\mathscr{U}[{\beta}={{\tau}={\epsilon}}]
,

\alpha\in\mathsf{fv}({\tau})
and
\alpha,\beta\mathop{\#}\mathsf{bv}({\mathscr{U}})
.
A unification problem
U
is said to be cyclic, written
\mathsf{cyclic}(U)
, if

\alpha\prec_{U}^{+}\alpha
for some
\alpha
.

Definition 5.1 (Solved form
\hat{U}
).

We write
\hat{U}
for constraints in
solved form
, that is,
constraints of the form
\exists{{\bar{\alpha}}}.\,\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}{\epsilon_{i}}
, where:

(1)

each
{\epsilon_{i}}
contains at most one non-variable type;

(2)

each type variable may occur as a member of at most one multi-equation

{\epsilon_{i}}
;

(3)

the constraint is acyclic.

5.2.
Administrative constraints

Section
Section

4
introduced the constraint language used for
constraint generation (
Section

4.2
). In this section we extend
the language with a small set of
administrative constraints
: auxiliary
constructs that never appear in generated constraints, but are introduced
internally by the solver to manage generalization and incremental
instantiation.

These administrative constraints enable the solver to represent and refine

partial type schemes
as solving progresses, capturing intermediate
generalization states and tracking how instantiations evolve as suspended
constraints are discharged.

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{?}{@{\extracolsep{\bnf@leftfill}}}}\begin{tabular*}{345.0pt}[]{.C?R=;;R;;LL~r.}&i&&&&Instantiation variables\\
&C&::=&\dots\;\mid\;\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]={C_{1}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{2}}\;\mid\;\exists{i^{x}}.\,C\;\mid\;{i}({\alpha})\leadsto{\tau}&&Constraints\\[4.30554pt]
&\mathfrak{r}&::=&({\mathfrak{g}},\phi)\qquad\qquad\!(\mathfrak{r}\in\mathcal{R})&&Ground regions\\
&\mathfrak{R}&\subseteq&\mathcal{R}&&Sets of ground regions\\
&\phi&::=&\dots\;\mid\;\phi[x:=\mathfrak{R}]\;\mid\;\phi[i:={\phi^{\prime}}]&&Semantic environments\end{tabular*}\par\phi(\lambda{{\alpha}[{\bar{\alpha}}]}.\,{C})\hskip 4.30554pt\mathrel{\triangleq}\hskip 4.30554pt\{({\mathfrak{g}},\phi[\alpha:={\mathfrak{g}},{\bar{\alpha}}:={\bar{\mathfrak{g}}}])\in\mathcal{R}:\phi[\alpha:={\mathfrak{g}},{\bar{\alpha}}:={\bar{\mathfrak{g}}}]\vdash C\}\par\frac{\mathfrak{R}=\phi(\lambda{{\alpha}[{\bar{\alpha}}]}.\,{{C_{1}}})\quad\mathfrak{R}\neq\emptyset\quad\quad\phi[x:=\mathfrak{R}]\vdash{C_{2}}}{\phi\vdash\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]={C_{1}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{2}}}\quad\textsc{LetR}\par\frac{(\phi(\tau),\mathord{\_})\in\phi(x)}{\phi\vdash x\;\tau}\quad\textsc{AppR}\par\frac{(\mathord{\_},{\phi^{\prime}})\in\phi(x)\quad\quad\phi[i:={\phi^{\prime}}]\vdash C}{\phi\vdash\exists{i^{x}}.\,C}\quad\textsc{Exists-Inst}\par\frac{\phi(i)(\alpha)=\phi(\tau)}{\phi\vdash{i}({\alpha})\leadsto{\tau}}\quad\textsc{Incr-Inst}

Figure 9
.
Syntax and semantics of region-based
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}
and
incremental instantiation constraints.

Regional let-constraints

As is standard in constraint-based formulations of
ML
type inference

(
29
)
, generalization-related rules operate on constraints
of the form
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x=\lambda{\alpha}.\,{\exists{\bar{\alpha}}.\,{C_{1}}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{2}}
. The outermost existentially
quantified variables
{\bar{\alpha}}
correspond to the
potentially generalizable

variables.
We refer to this existential prefix
\exists{\bar{\alpha}}
as a
region
.
Intuitively, a region delimits the scope of variables that may be generalized
once the abstraction has been fully solved.

To make regions explicit, we introduce regional let-constraints
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]={C_{1}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{2}}
(
Figure

9
), where
\alpha
is the

root
of the region and
{\bar{\alpha}}
are auxiliary existential variables.
The order of
{\bar{\alpha}}
is immaterial; regions are considered equal
up to permutation of these variables.

Satisfiability of regional let-constraints is defined in

Figure

9
. The semantics of an abstraction with a
region, written
\phi(\lambda{{\alpha}[{\bar{\alpha}}]}.\,{C})
, is a set
\mathfrak{R}
of

ground regions
that satisfy
C
. A ground region
\mathfrak{r}
is a pair
({\mathfrak{g}},\phi)
; it satisfies the constraint abstraction
\lambda{{\alpha}[{\bar{\alpha}}]}.\,{C}
if

\phi
satisfies
C
, and
{\mathfrak{g}}
is
\phi(\alpha)
. Intuitively, the region
environment
\phi
carries a ground type for each inference variable
\beta

occurring in
C
; in particular, a ground instance for
\alpha
and
{\bar{\alpha}}
.

The rules
LetR
and
AppR
are the
regional counterparts
of
the
Let
and
App
rules introduced in
Section

4
. They
are semantically equivalent to their non-regional forms, except that each
operates over sets of ground regions (
\mathfrak{R}
) rather than sets of ground
types (
\mathfrak{S}
). The additional environment
\phi
carried by these ground
regions
({\mathfrak{g}},\phi)
is semantically inert for
LetR
and

AppR
themselves, but will become important later, in the semantics of

incremental instantiation
constraints (
Section

5.2
).

Regional let-constraints strictly generalize ordinary let-constraints, as
captured by the equivalence:

\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x=\lambda{\alpha}.\,{\exists{\bar{\alpha}}.\,{C_{1}}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{2}}\hskip 4.30554pt\equiv\hskip 4.30554pt\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]={C_{1}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{2}}

The trouble with lets

Solving let-constraints is deceptively difficult.
Naively, let-constraints (or
generalization
constraints) could be
solved by copying constraints:

S-Let-App-Beta

\hfil\frac{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]={C_{1}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}\mathscr{C}[x\;\tau]\quad\alpha,{\bar{\alpha}}\mathop{\#}\tau\quad x\mathop{\#}\mathsf{bv}({\mathscr{C}})}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]={C_{1}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}\mathscr{C}[\exists{\alpha,{\bar{\alpha}}}.\,{\alpha}={\tau}\wedge{C_{1}}]}

This rule, due to
29
, resembles
\beta
-reduction in
some presentations of explicit substitutions. While this rule is sound
when
{C_{1}}
is a
simple
constraint, it becomes unsound for abstractions
containing suspended constraints.

To see why, consider again the example
ex_8
from
Section

2.4
:

⬇

type

’
a

gpoint

=

{

x

:

’
a
;

y

:

’
a

}

let

diag

(
n

:

’
a
)

:

’
a

gpoint

=

{

x

=

n
;

y

=

n

}

°\
halfline
°

let

ex_8

gp

=

let

getx

p

=

p
.
x

in

getx

(
diag

42),

(
getx

gp

:

float
)

°\
ocamlflags

20°

As explained earlier, this program is clearly well-typed: the type of
p

is unambiguously determined as
’b

gpoint
through
backpropagation

from the first application of
getx
(
getx

(
diag

42)
).
The (simplified) generated constraint for
ex_8
contains:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\exists{{\alpha_{\texttt{gp}}},\delta}.&\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}{\texttt{getx}}=\lambda{{\alpha_{\texttt{getx}}}}.\,{\exists{{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}},\gamma}.\,\bigwedge\left(\begin{tabular}[]{L}{{\alpha_{\texttt{getx}}}}={{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}}\to\gamma}\\
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}}\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}(\mathsf{rcd}\;{t}\;{\mathord{\_}})\Rightarrow t.\texttt{x}\leq{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}}\to\gamma\end{tabular}\right)}\\[8.61108pt]
&\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{{\texttt{getx}}\;(\mathsf{rcd}\;{\texttt{gpoint}}\;{\mathsf{int}}\to\delta)\wedge{\texttt{getx}}\;({\alpha_{\texttt{gp}}}\to\mathsf{float})}\end{array}

If we now apply
S-Let-App-Beta
to both applications of
getx

(removing the let-constraint for concision), we obtain:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\exists{{\alpha_{\texttt{gp}}},\delta}.&\exists{{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}}_{1},\gamma_{1}}.\,\bigwedge\left(\begin{tabular}[]{L}{\mathsf{rcd}\;{\texttt{gpoint}}\;{\mathsf{int}}\to\delta}={{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}}_{1}\to\gamma_{1}}\\
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}}_{1}\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}(\mathsf{rcd}\;{t}\;{\mathord{\_}})\Rightarrow t.\texttt{x}\leq{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}}_{1}\to\gamma_{1}\end{tabular}\right)\\
\wedge&\exists{{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}}_{2},\gamma_{2}}.\,\bigwedge\left(\begin{tabular}[]{L}{{\alpha_{\texttt{gp}}}\to\mathsf{float}}={{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}}_{2}\to\gamma_{2}}\\
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}}_{2}\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}(\mathsf{rcd}\;{t}\;{\mathord{\_}})\Rightarrow t.\texttt{x}\leq{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}}_{2}\to\gamma_{2}\end{tabular}\right)\end{array}

The first conjunct is satisfiable, since
{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}}_{1}
is unified with
\mathsf{rcd}\;{\texttt{gpoint}}\;{\mathsf{int}}
, thereby discharging the match constraint on
{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}}_{1}
. The
second, however, is not:
{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}}_{2}
remains underdetermined, leaving its match
constraint unsatisfiable. Thus, although the original constraint was
satisfiable, the application of
S-Let-App-Beta
makes it
unsatisfiable. By copying the abstraction, we lose the essential

sharing
between both instantiations—namely, that both copies of

{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}}
(
{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}}_{1}
and
{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}}_{2}
) must have the same shape
\nu\gamma.\,\mathsf{rcd}\;{\texttt{gpoint}}\;{\gamma}
. This loss of sharing is precisely why
S-Let-App-Beta

is unsound in the presence of suspended constraints.

A tempting alternative, used in

42
and

1
, is to treat the let-bindings

monomorphically
, sharing
{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}}
directly between both applications.
However, this is incomplete: in
ex_8
, the two calls to
getx

require different instantiations of
{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}}
—
int

gpoint
and
float
gpoint
, respectively.

Even setting soundness aside,
S-Let-App-Beta
is inefficient. Each
application duplicates constraint solving work for the same abstraction.
A more efficient approach is to
solve once and reuse
: first solve the
abstraction once—
e.g.
reducing it to
\lambda{{\alpha}[{\bar{\alpha}}]}.\,{{\bar{\epsilon}}}
, where
{\bar{\alpha}}
are
generalizable variables—and then reuse the result at each instantiation site
by only copying the solved constraint
\epsilon
. This mirrors the generalization
and instantiation steps of
ML
inference algorithms such as
\mathcal{W}
:

\lambda{{\alpha}[{\bar{\alpha}}]}.\,{{\bar{\epsilon}}}
corresponds to the type scheme
\forall{{\bar{\alpha}}}.\,{\vartheta(\alpha)}
, where
\vartheta
is the most general unifier of
{\bar{\epsilon}}
.

29
formalize this connection, and the optimized
treatment is naturally expressed as a strategy on top of their

S-Let-App-Beta
rule.

We therefore face two related challenges:

(1)

handle instantiation soundly in the presence of suspended constraints, and

(2)

to avoid redundant work by reusing
partial
results across instantiations.

To address both, we introduce
partial type schemes
, our second novel
mechanism for omnidirectional inference. Partial type schemes are type schemes
that delay commitment to certain quantifications (
e.g.

{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}}
and
\gamma
).
Such
partially generalized
variables are treated as generalized, but can
be incrementally refined in future as suspended constraints are discharged.

Returning to our running example, we begin with the partial type scheme
\forall{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}},\gamma.\,{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}}\to\gamma
for
getx
, since the suspended
match constraint in its abstraction cannot yet be solved. We continue solving
the body of the let-constraint, tracking every instances of this partial
scheme. As type information flows back—
e.g.
when the shape of
{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}}
becomes
known via backpropagation from the first application of
getx
—the
scheme is refined to
\forall\beta.\,\mathsf{rcd}\;{\texttt{gpoint}}\;{\beta}\to\beta
. The second
application of
getx
then updates its instantiation accordingly, unifying

{\alpha_{\texttt{p}}}_{2}
with
\mathsf{rcd}\;{\texttt{gpoint}}\;{\beta}_{2}
and
\gamma_{2}
with
\beta_{2}
.

Incremental instantiation

To support partial type schemes, we also extend the constraint language with

incremental instantiation constraints

(
Figure

9
).
We introduce two new constraint formers:

(1)

\exists{i^{x}}.\,C
, which binds a fresh instantiation
i
of
x
’s
region within
C
, and

(2)

{i}({\alpha})\leadsto{\tau}
, which asserts that the copy of
\alpha
in
i

equals
\tau
.

The instantiation variable
i
is required to ensure all incremental
instantiations
{i}({\alpha})\leadsto{\tau}
are solved uniformly.

Within the solver, we view incremental instantiations as markers indicating
which parts of the abstraction still need to be copied, and abstractions
themselves are treated as partial type schemes.

This mechanism enables efficient handling of constraint instantiations: solved
parts are reused immediately, while suspended constraints can be solved later,
further refining the abstraction and propagating new equations to all
instantiation sites.

We now turn to the semantics of incremental instantiations
(
Figure

9
). The existential constraint
\exists{i^{x}}.\,C
is satisfiable (
Exists-Inst
) if
C
is satisfiable with

i
bound to one of the region environments
{\phi^{\prime}}
in the
interpretation of
x
.
An incremental instantiation
{i}({\alpha})\leadsto{\tau}
is satisfiable
(
Incr-Inst
) exactly when
i
’s instance of
\alpha

(
i.e.
,
\phi(i)(\alpha)
) is equal to
\tau
.

When a regional abstraction
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]={C_{1}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{2}}
contains an
incremental instantiation constraint
{i}({\beta})\leadsto{\tau}
, and
i
is
an instantiation of
x
, the variable
\beta
may range over any free variable
of
{C_{1}}
—including
\alpha
and the regional variables
{\bar{\alpha}}
. In other
words, the regional let-constraint
{\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]={C_{1}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{2}}}
provides a
non-obvious scoping rule: the variables
\alpha
and
{\bar{\alpha}}
are also bound in

{C_{2}}
, but may occur there only within incremental instantiations of

x
. This subtlety justifies introducing an explicit syntax for regional
let-constraints, rather than encoding them as
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x=\lambda{\alpha}.\,{\exists{\bar{\alpha}}.\,{C_{1}}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{2}}
.

5.3.
Solving rules

We now gradually introduce the rules of the constraint solver itself
(
Figures

10
,
11
and
12
;
for a complete view with all rules placed together, see Appendix
Appendix

A
).
These rules define a non-deterministic rewriting
system, operating modulo
\alpha
-equivalence, and the associativity and
commutativity of conjunction. Rewriting takes place under an arbitrary
one-hole constraint context
\mathscr{C}
.
A constraint
C
is satisfiable if it rewrites to a solved form
\hat{U}

(
Definition

5.1
); otherwise it gets stuck—in particular,
\mathsf{false}
is
considered a stuck constraint.

Basic rules

\frac{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{U_{1}}\quad{U_{1}}\longrightarrow{U_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{U_{2}}}\quad\textsc{S-Unif}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathscr{C}[\mathsf{false}]\quad\mathscr{C}\neq\square}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{false}}\quad\textsc{S-False}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x=\lambda{\alpha}.\,{{C_{1}}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{2}}\quad}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[\emptyset]={C_{1}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{2}}}\quad\textsc{S-Let}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\exists{\alpha}.\,{C_{1}})\wedge{C_{2}}\quad\alpha\mathop{\#}{C_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\exists{\alpha}.\,{C_{1}}\wedge{C_{2}}}\quad\textsc{S-Exists-Conj}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]=\exists{\beta}.\,{C_{1}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{2}}\quad\beta\mathop{\#}\alpha,{\bar{\alpha}},{C_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}},\beta]={C_{1}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{2}}}\quad\textsc{S-Let-ExistsLeft}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]={C_{1}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}\exists{\beta}.\,{C_{2}}\quad\beta\mathop{\#}\alpha,{\bar{\alpha}},{C_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\exists{\beta}.\,\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]={C_{1}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{2}}}\quad\textsc{S-Let-ExistsRight}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]={C_{1}}\wedge{C_{2}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{3}}\quad{C_{1}}\mathop{\#}\alpha,{\bar{\alpha}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{C_{1}}\wedge\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]={C_{2}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{3}}}\quad\textsc{S-Let-ConjLeft}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]={C_{1}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}({C_{2}}\wedge{C_{3}})\quad x\mathop{\#}{C_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{C_{2}}\wedge\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]={C_{1}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{3}}}\quad\textsc{S-Let-ConjRight}

Figure 10
.
Basic rewriting rules
{C_{1}}\longrightarrow{C_{2}}
.

Figure

10
contains a selected set of basic solving rules, drawn
from the standard repertoire of small-step constraint solvers for
ML
type inference.

S-Unif
invokes the unification algorithm on the
current unification problem. The unification algorithm itself is treated as a
black box by the solver, so the system could be extended with any
equational theory of types implemented by the unification algorithm.

S-Let
rewrites let-constraints into regional form.

S-Exists-Conj
lifts existentials across conjunctions;

S-Let-ExistsLeft
and
S-Let-ExistsRight
lift existentials across
let-binders;
S-Let-ConjLeft
,
S-Let-ConjRight
hoist constraints
out of let-binders when they are independent of the local variables.
Collectively, these lifting rules normalize the structure of each region into a
block of existentially bound variables, whose body consists of a conjunction of
solved multi-equations followed by a residual constraint—typically an
instantiation, let-binding, or suspended constraint.

OmniML
-specific constraints, such as the label and polytype instantiation
constraints (
t.\ell\leq{\tau_{1}}\to{\tau_{2}}
,
s\leq\tau
,

etc.
), require no special treatment in our solver. Once their pattern variables
are substituted—after solving a match constraint—they are desugared into
constraints already handled by the solver.

\frac{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]=C\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}\mathscr{C}[{{i}^{x}}({\beta})\leadsto{\gamma}]\quad\quad\neg\mathsf{cyclic}({C})\quad x\mathop{\#}\mathsf{bv}({\mathscr{C}})\quad\quad C={C^{\prime}}\wedge{\beta}={{\varsigma\,{\bar{\beta}}}={\epsilon}}\quad\beta\in\alpha,{\bar{\alpha}}\quad{\bar{\beta}}^{\prime}\mathop{\#}\beta,\gamma,{\bar{\beta}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]=C\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}\mathscr{C}[\exists{{\bar{\beta}}^{\prime}}.\,{\gamma}={\varsigma\,{\bar{\beta}}^{\prime}}\wedge{{i}^{x}}({{\bar{\beta}}})\leadsto{{\bar{\beta}}^{\prime}}]\end{array}}}\quad\textsc{S-Inst-Copy}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{i}({\beta})\leadsto{{\gamma_{1}}}\wedge{i}({\beta})\leadsto{{\gamma_{2}}}\quad}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{i}({\beta})\leadsto{{\gamma_{1}}}\wedge{{\gamma_{1}}}={{\gamma_{2}}}}\quad\textsc{S-Inst-Unify}\par\frac{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]={\bar{\epsilon}}\wedge
