Theorem C.14 (Progress).

If constraint
C
cannot take a step
C\longrightarrow{C^{\prime}}
, then either:

(1)

C
is solved.

(2)

C
is stuck, it is either:

(a)

\mathsf{false}
;

(b)

\hat{\mathscr{C}}[x\;\tau]
where
x\mathop{\#}\hat{\mathscr{C}}
;

(c)

\hat{\mathscr{C}}[{{i}^{x}}({\alpha})\leadsto{\gamma}]
where

x\mathop{\#}\hat{\mathscr{C}}
and
i(\alpha)\mathop{\#}\mathsf{insts}(\hat{\mathscr{C}})
;

(d)

for every match constraint
\hat{\mathscr{C}}[\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\alpha\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}{\bar{\chi}}]
in

C
,
\hat{\mathscr{C}}[\alpha\mathop{!}\varsigma]
does not hold for any
\varsigma
.

Here,
\hat{\mathscr{C}}
is a normal context,
i.e.
, such that no other
rewrites can be applied.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the structure of
C
. We
focus on suspended match constraints, conjunctions, and
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}
rules.

1

\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\tau\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}{\bar{\chi}}
.
We have two cases:

1

\tau
is a non-variable type
. Apply
S-Match-Ctx
using
S-Uni-Type

2

\tau
is a type variable
\alpha
.

We have
\square[\alpha\mathop{\hbox to0.0pt{$\diagdown$\hss}\diagup\!\!\!{!}\,}]
. It suffices
that every match constraint in a context-reachable position

\hat{\mathscr{C}}[\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}{\alpha^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}{\bar{\chi}}]
satisfies
\hat{\mathscr{C}}[{\alpha^{\prime}}\mathop{\hbox to0.0pt{$\diagdown$\hss}\diagup\!\!\!{!}\,}]
.
By the definition of constraint contexts, there is only one such

\hat{\mathscr{C}}
, namely
\square
, for which we already have
\square[\alpha\mathop{\hbox to0.0pt{$\diagdown$\hss}\diagup\!\!\!{!}\,}]
.
Hence
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\tau\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}{\bar{\chi}}
is stuck.

2

{C_{1}}\wedge{C_{2}}
.
We begin by inducting on
{C_{1}}
and
{C_{2}}
. Then we consider cases:

1

{C_{1}}
(or
{C_{2}}
) take a step
. Apply congruence rewriting rule.

2

{C_{1}}
(or
{C_{2}}
) is
\mathsf{true}
. Apply
S-True
.

3

{C_{1}}
(or
{C_{2}}
) is
\mathsf{false}
. Apply
S-False
.

4

{C_{1}}
(or
{C_{2}}
) begins with
\exists
. Apply
S-Exists-Conj
.

5

{C_{1}},{C_{2}}
are solved
.

We either apply the above
\exists
case, or both
{C_{1}}
and
{C_{2}}
are solved
multi-equations
{\bar{\epsilon}}_{1},{\bar{\epsilon}}_{2}
. We perform cases on this:

1

{\bar{\epsilon}}_{1}
and
{\bar{\epsilon}}_{2}
are mergable
. Apply
U-Merge
.

2

\mathsf{cyclic}({\bar{\epsilon}}_{1},{\bar{\epsilon}}_{2})
. Apply
U-Cycle
.

3

Otherwise
. The conjunction
{\bar{\epsilon}}_{1}\wedge{\bar{\epsilon}}_{2}
is solved.

6

{C_{1}}
and
{C_{2}}
are stuck (and not
\mathsf{false}
)
.

w.l.o.g.
, consider cases
{C_{1}}
.

1

\hat{\mathscr{C}_{1}}[x\;\tau]
.
We have
x\mathop{\#}\mathsf{bv}({\hat{\mathscr{C}_{1}}})
.

\hat{\mathscr{C}_{1}}[x\;\tau]\wedge{C_{2}}
is stuck as we do not bind
x
in
\hat{\mathscr{C}_{1}}\wedge{C_{2}}
.

2

\hat{\mathscr{C}_{1}}[{{i}^{x}}({\alpha})\leadsto{\gamma}]
.
We have
x\mathop{\#}\mathsf{bv}({\hat{\mathscr{C}_{1}}})
and
i(\alpha)\mathop{\#}\mathsf{insts}(\hat{\mathscr{C}_{1}})
.

If
i(\alpha)\in\mathsf{insts}({C_{2}})
and
i\mathop{\#}\mathsf{bv}({\hat{C_{1}}})
, then apply
S-Inst-Unify
.
It must be the case that we can apply
S-Inst-Unify
, otherwise, we could lift these instantiation
constraints using
S-Exists-Lower
and
S-Let-ConjLeft
, contradicting that
\hat{\mathscr{C}_{1}}
is stuck.

Otherwise,
x\mathop{\#}\mathsf{bv}({\hat{\mathscr{C}_{1}}\wedge{C_{2}}})
, thus
\hat{\mathscr{C}_{1}}[{{i}^{x}}({\alpha})\leadsto{\gamma}]
is stuck.

3

\hat{\mathscr{C}_{1}}[\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}{\alpha^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}{\bar{\chi}}]
.
We have
{\mathscr{C}_{1}}[{\alpha^{\prime}}\mathop{\hbox to0.0pt{$\diagdown$\hss}\diagup\!\!\!{!}\,}]
.

Consider a match constraint
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}{\alpha^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}{\bar{\chi}}
in
{C_{1}}
.

If
\vdash[\hat{\mathscr{C}_{1}}[-]\wedge{C_{2}}][{\alpha^{\prime}}\mathop{!}\varsigma]
. Then we can apply
S-Match-Ctx
.

Otherwise
\not\vdash[\hat{\mathscr{C}_{1}}[-]\wedge{C_{2}}][{\alpha^{\prime}}\mathop{!}\varsigma]
. We have
Lemma

C.11
,
so we are stuck and
({\mathscr{C}_{1}}\wedge{C_{2}})[{\alpha^{\prime}}\mathop{\hbox to0.0pt{$\diagdown$\hss}\diagup\!\!\!{!}\,}]
.

3

\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]={C_{1}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{2}}
.
We begin by inducting on
{C_{1}}
and
{C_{2}}
. Then we consider cases:

1

{C_{1}}
(or
{C_{2}}
) take a step
. Apply congruence rewriting rule.

2

{C_{1}}
(or
{C_{2}}
) is
\mathsf{false}
. Apply
S-False
.

3

{C_{1}}
begins with
\exists
. Apply
S-Let-ExistsLeft

4

{C_{2}}
begins with
\exists
. Apply
S-Let-ExistsRight

5

{C_{2}}
begins with
\wedge
with
x\mathop{\#}
from conjunct
. Apply
S-Let-ConjRight
.

6

{C_{1}}
begins with
\wedge
with
\alpha,{\bar{\alpha}}\mathop{\#}
from conjunct
. Try apply
S-Let-ConjLeft

7

{C_{2}}
begins with
\exists{i^{{x^{\prime}}}}.\,
,
x\neq{x^{\prime}}
. Apply
S-Exists-Inst-Let

8

{\alpha^{\prime}}\in{\bar{\alpha}}
is determined by
{C_{1}}
. Apply
S-Exists-Lower

9

{C_{2}}
is solved
.

Thus
{C_{2}}
must be
\mathsf{true}
(due to above cases).

1

{C_{1}}
is solved
.
Thus
{C_{1}}
must be
{\bar{\epsilon}}
.

There are two cases:

•

\exists{\alpha,{\bar{\alpha}}}.\,{\bar{\epsilon}}\equiv\mathsf{true}
. Apply
S-Let-Solve
.

•

\exists{\alpha,{\bar{\alpha}}}.\,{\bar{\epsilon}}\nequiv\mathsf{true}
. It must be the case there is some
\beta
that dominates a
{\alpha^{\prime}}
in
\alpha,{\bar{\alpha}}
in
{\bar{\epsilon}}
.
Hence
\exists{\alpha,{\bar{\alpha}}\setminus{\alpha^{\prime}}}.\,{\bar{\epsilon}}\mathrel{\mathsf{determines}}{\alpha^{\prime}}
.
So we can apply
S-Exists-Lower
.

2

{C_{1}}
is stuck
.

The constraint
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]={C_{1}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{2}}
remains stuck, since
no additional term variable bindings occur for the scope of
{C_{1}}
,
ruling out the instantiation cases. Additionally, we cannot apply
backpropagation since
{C_{2}}
is
\mathsf{true}
.

10

{C_{2}}
is stuck
.

1

\hat{\mathscr{C}}[x\;\tau]
. We have
x\mathop{\#}\mathsf{bv}({\hat{\mathscr{C}}})
.

Apply
S-Let-AppR
.

2

\hat{\mathscr{C}}[{{i}^{x}}({{\alpha^{\prime}}})\leadsto{\gamma}]
. We have
x\mathop{\#}\mathsf{bv}({\hat{\mathscr{C}}})
or
i({\alpha^{\prime}})\mathop{\#}\mathsf{insts}(\hat{\mathscr{C}})
.

•

{\alpha^{\prime}}\in\alpha,{\bar{\alpha}}
.

We can either apply
S-Inst-Copy
or
S-Compress

if a multi-equation involving
{\alpha^{\prime}}
occurs in
{C_{1}}
.

Otherwise, we consider cases where
{C_{1}}
is solved or stuck.

If
{C_{1}}
is solved, then it must be of the form
{\bar{\epsilon}}
.
There are two cases:

–

\exists{\alpha,{\bar{\alpha}}}.\,{\bar{\epsilon}}\equiv\mathsf{true}
.
As
{\alpha^{\prime}}
does not appear in the head position of any multi-equation in
{\bar{\epsilon}}
,
it must be polymorphic. Thus
\forall{{\alpha^{\prime}}}.\,\exists{\alpha,{\bar{\alpha}}\setminus{\alpha^{\prime}}}.\,{\bar{\epsilon}}\equiv\mathsf{true}
.
So we can apply
S-Inst-Poly
.

–

\exists{\alpha,{\bar{\alpha}}}.\,{\bar{\epsilon}}\nequiv\mathsf{true}
.
Apply
S-Exists-Lower
(using the same logic as above).

If
{C_{1}}
is stuck, then neither stuck case regarding instantiations
in
{C_{1}}
is fixed, so in these cases the constraint remains
stuck. If
{C_{1}}
is stuck with
\hat{\mathscr{C}^{\prime}}[\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\beta\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}{\bar{\chi}}^{\prime}]
. Then either backpropagation (via
S-Uni-BackProp
and
S-Match-Ctx
)
applies with an equation in
\hat{\mathscr{C}}
, or the entire constraint
is stuck (by
Lemma

C.11
).

•

{\alpha^{\prime}}\notin\alpha,{\bar{\alpha}}
. Apply
S-Inst-Mono
.

3

For any
\hat{\mathscr{C}}[\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}{\alpha^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}{\bar{\chi}}]
. We have
\hat{\mathscr{C}}[{\alpha^{\prime}}\mathop{\hbox to0.0pt{$\diagdown$\hss}\diagup\!\!\!{!}\,}]
.

Either
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]={C_{1}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{2}}
can progress with an instantiation constraint (in the above case) to discharge
the match constraint or
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]={C_{1}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{2}}
is stuck.

∎

C.3.
Termination

This section presents a proof of termination for our solver.
Most rewrite rules, in both unification and constraint solving, are

destructive
—that is, they eliminate or modify the structure of a
constraint in a way that prevents the rule from begin applied again.
Consequently, to establish termination, it suffices to consider only those
rules that are not inherently destructive.

Lemma C.15 (Unification termination).

The unifier terminates on all inputs.

Proof.

Let every shape
\varsigma
have an integer
weight

defined by
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{sw}}{(\varsigma)}\mathrel{\triangleq}4+2\times|\varsigma|
, where
|\varsigma|
is the
arity of the shape
\varsigma
.
The weight of a type
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{tw}}{(\tau)}
is defined by:

{}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{tw}}{(\alpha)}{}

{}\mathrel{\triangleq}{}

{}1{}

{}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{tw}}{(\varsigma\,{\bar{\tau}})}{}

{}\mathrel{\triangleq}{}

{}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{iw}}{(\varsigma\,{\bar{\tau}})}-2{}

{}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{iw}}{(\alpha)}{}

{}\mathrel{\triangleq}{}

{}0{}

{}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{iw}}{(\varsigma\,{\bar{\tau}})}{}

{}\mathrel{\triangleq}{}

{}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{sw}}{(\varsigma)}+\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{iw}}{({\bar{\tau}})}{}

{}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{iw}}{({\bar{\tau}})}{}

{}\mathrel{\triangleq}{}

{}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{iw}}{({\tau_{i}})}{}

The helper
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{iw}}{(\tau)}
computes the “internal” weight of
\tau
; in
the common case of shallow types it is just the weight of its head
shape.

We define the weight of a multi-equation as the sum of the weights of its
members. The weight of a unification problem
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{uw}}{(U)}
is defined
as the sum of the weights of its multi-equations.

In
U\longrightarrow{U^{\prime}}
, the rules
U-Decomp
and
U-Name
are not
obviously destructive, as they may introduce new constraints that
are structurally larger than the constraint being rewritten.

However, we show that this is not problematic: in both cases, the unification
weight
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{uw}}{(U)}
strictly decreases. The remaining rules are obviously
destructive and either maintain or decrease the unification weight.

1

{\frac{{\varsigma\,{\bar{\alpha}}}={{\varsigma\,{\bar{\beta}}}={\epsilon}}}{{\varsigma\,{\bar{\alpha}}}={\epsilon}\wedge{{\bar{\alpha}}}={{\bar{\beta}}}}\quad\textsc{fraction={{}{-}\to},Right=U-Decomp}}

We have:

{}(+){}

{}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{uw}}{({\varsigma\,{\bar{\alpha}}}={{\varsigma\,{\bar{\beta}}}={\epsilon}})}{}

{}={}

{}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{tw}}{(\varsigma\,{\bar{\alpha}})}+\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{tw}}{(\varsigma\,{\bar{\beta}})}+\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{tw}}{(\epsilon)}{}

{}(-){}

{}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{uw}}{({\varsigma\,{\bar{\alpha}}}={\epsilon}\wedge{{\bar{\alpha}}}={{\bar{\beta}}})}{}

{}={}

{}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{tw}}{(\varsigma\,{\bar{\alpha}})}+\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{tw}}{(\epsilon)}+\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{tw}}{({\bar{\alpha}})}+\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{tw}}{({\bar{\beta}})}\raise 3.0pt\lower 1.5pt{}

{}={}

{}\raise 3.0pt\lower 1.5pt\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{tw}}{(\varsigma\,{\bar{\beta}})}-\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{tw}}{({\bar{\alpha}})}-\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{tw}}{({\bar{\beta}})}{}

{}={}

{}(\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{sw}}{(\varsigma)}+0-2)-2|\varsigma|{}

{}={}

{}(2+2|\varsigma|)-2|\varsigma|\hskip 4.30554pt=\hskip 4.30554pt\textbf{2}{}

Hence
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{uw}}{({{\varsigma\,{\bar{\alpha}}}={{\varsigma\,{\bar{\beta}}}={\epsilon}}})}>\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{uw}}{({\varsigma\,{\bar{\alpha}}}={\epsilon}\wedge{{\bar{\alpha}}}={{\bar{\beta}}})}
.

2

{\frac{{\varsigma\,({\bar{\tau}},{\tau_{i}},{\bar{\tau}^{\prime}})}={\epsilon}\hskip 8.19447pt\alpha\mathop{\#}{\bar{\tau}},{\bar{\tau}^{\prime}},\epsilon\hskip 8.19447pt{\tau_{i}}\notin\mathcal{V}}{\exists{\alpha}.\,{\varsigma\,({\bar{\tau}},\alpha,{\bar{\tau}^{\prime}})}={\epsilon}\wedge{\alpha}={{\tau_{i}}}}\quad\textsc{fraction={{}{-}\to},Right=U-Name}}

Given
{\tau_{i}}\notin\mathcal{V}
, by
Theorem

3.2
,

{\tau_{i}}={\varsigma^{\prime}}\,\bar{\tau^{\prime\prime}}
for some shape
{\varsigma^{\prime}}
and types
\bar{\tau^{\prime\prime}}
.
So we have:

{}(+){}

{}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{uw}}{({\varsigma\,{({\bar{\tau}},{\tau_{i}},{\bar{\tau}^{\prime}})}}={\epsilon})}{}

{}={}

{}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{sw}}{(\varsigma)}+\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{iw}}{({\bar{\tau}})}+\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{iw}}{({\tau_{i}})}+\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{iw}}{({\bar{\tau}^{\prime}})}-2+\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{uw}}{(\epsilon)}{}

{}(-){}

{}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{uw}}{(\exists{\alpha}.\,{\alpha}={{\tau_{i}}}\wedge{\varsigma\,({\bar{\tau}},\alpha,{\bar{\tau}^{\prime}})}={\epsilon})}{}

{}={}

{}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{sw}}{(\varsigma)}+\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{iw}}{({\bar{\tau}})}+0+\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{iw}}{({\bar{\tau}^{\prime}})}-2+\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{uw}}{(\epsilon)}+1+\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{tw}}{({\tau_{i}})}{}

{}={}

{}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{iw}}{({\tau_{i}})}-\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{iw}}{(\alpha)}-\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{tw}}{({\tau_{i}})}-1{}

{}={}

{}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{iw}}{({\tau_{i}})}-0-(\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{iw}}{({\tau_{i}})}-2)-1{}

{}={}

1

Hence
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{uw}}{({\varsigma\,({\bar{\tau}},{\tau_{i}},{\bar{\tau}^{\prime}})}={\epsilon})}>\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{uw}}{(\exists{\alpha}.\,{\varsigma\,({\bar{\tau}},\alpha,{\bar{\tau}^{\prime}})}={\epsilon}\wedge{\alpha}={{\tau_{i}}})}
.

∎

See
5.6

Proof.

The difficulty for termination comes from the “suspended match discharge” rule

S-Match-Ctx
which can make arbitrary
sub-constraints appear in the non-suspended part of the constraint;
and from the instantiation rules that copy/duplicate existing
structure in another part of the constraint, increasing its total
size.

As we argued before, the other rewrite rules are
destructive
,
they strictly simplify the constraint towards a normal form and can
only be applied finitely many times when taken together. The
fragment without discharge rules and incremental instantiation is
also extremely similar to the constraint language of

29
, so their termination proof applies
directly.

Discharge rules

The discharge rules strictly decrease
the number of occurrences of suspended match constraint (if we also
count nested suspended constraints), and no rewriting rule
introduces new suspended match constraints. So these discharge rules
can only be applied finitely many times. To prove termination of
constraint solving, it thus suffices to prove that rewriting
sequences that do not contain one of the discharge rules (those that
occur in-between two discharge rules) are always finite.

Starting instantiations

By a similar argument, the number
of non-incremental instantiations
x\;\tau
decreases strictly on

S-Let-AppR
when an incremental instantiation starts, and is
preserved by other non-discharge rules. The rule
S-Let-AppR

can thus only occur finitely many times in non-discharging sequences,
and it suffices to prove that all rewriting sequences that are
non-discharging and do not contain
S-Let-AppR
are finite.

Other instantiation rules

Among other instantiation
rules, the rule of concern is
S-Inst-Copy
, which is not
destructive: it introduces new instantiation constraints
and structurally increases the size of the constraint.

Intuitively,
S-Inst-Copy
should not endanger termination
because the amount of copying it can perform for a given
instantiation is bounded by the size of the types in the constraint

C
it is copying from. (
C
could have cyclic equations with
infinite unfoldings, but
S-Inst-Copy
forbids copying in that
case.) The difficulty is that rewrites to
C
can be interleaved
with instantiation rules, so that the equations that are being
copied can grow strictly during instantiation.

To control this, we perform a structural induction: to prove that

(\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]={C_{1}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{2}})
does not contain infinite
non-discharging non-instance-starting rewrite rules, we can assume
that the result holds for the strictly smaller constraint
{C_{1}}
, and
then prove termination of the incremental instantiations of
x
in

{C_{2}}
. (The notion of structural size used here is preserved by
non-discharging rewrite rules, as they do not affect the
let-structure of the constraint.)

Assuming that
{C_{1}}
has no infinite rewriting sequence, it suffices
to prove that only finitely many rewrites in the rest of the
constraint (namely
{C_{2}}
) can occur between each rewrite of
{C_{1}}
.

We define a weight that captures the contribution of types
within
{C_{1}}
to the partial instances in
{C_{2}}
:

{}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{tw}}{(\varsigma\,{\bar{\tau}})}{}

{}\mathrel{\triangleq}{}

{}2\times\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{sw}}{(\varsigma)}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{tw}}{({\tau_{i}})}{}

{}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{tw}}{(\alpha)}{}

{}\mathrel{\triangleq}{}

{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sup\left\{\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{tw}}{(\tau)}:{\alpha}={\tau}\in{C_{1}}\right\}&\text{if ${C_{1}}$ is acyclic}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.{}

The weight of a incremental instantiation
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{cw}}{({{i}^{x}}({\alpha})\leadsto{\tau})}
is
defined as the sum of
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{tw}}{(\tau)}
and
{\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{tw}}{(\alpha)}}
.
The weight of other constraints is given using the measure

\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{uw}}
defined in the the
proof of
Lemma

C.15
.

1

{\frac{\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]=C\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}\mathscr{C}[{{i}^{x}}({{\alpha^{\prime}}})\leadsto{\gamma}]\hskip 8.19447ptC={C^{\prime}}\wedge{{\alpha^{\prime}}}={{\varsigma\,{\bar{\beta}}}={\epsilon}}\hskip 8.19447pt{\alpha^{\prime}}\in\alpha,{\bar{\alpha}}\hskip 8.19447pt\neg\mathsf{cyclic}(C)\hskip 8.19447pt{\bar{\beta}}^{\prime}\mathop{\#}{\alpha^{\prime}},\gamma,{\bar{\beta}}\hskip 8.19447ptx\mathop{\#}\mathsf{bv}({\mathscr{C}})}{\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x\;\alpha\;[{\bar{\alpha}}]=C\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}\mathscr{C}[\exists{{\bar{\beta}}^{\prime}}.\,{\gamma}={\varsigma\,{\bar{\beta}}^{\prime}}\wedge{{i}^{x}}({{\bar{\beta}}})\leadsto{{\bar{\beta}}^{\prime}}]}\quad\textsc{fraction={{}{-}\to},Right=S-Inst-Copy}}

We aim to show that the weight of the rewritten constraint

\exists{{\bar{\beta}}^{\prime}}.\,{\gamma}={\varsigma\,{\bar{\beta}}^{\prime}}\wedge{{i}^{x}}({{\bar{\beta}}})\leadsto{{\bar{\beta}}^{\prime}}

is strictly less than the original
{{i}^{x}}({{\alpha^{\prime}}})\leadsto{\gamma}
.

{}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{cw}}{({{i}^{x}}({{\alpha^{\prime}}})\leadsto{\gamma})}{}

{}={}

{}1+\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{tw}}{(\alpha)}{}

{}\geq{}

{}1+2\times\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{sw}}{(\varsigma)}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{tw}}{({\beta_{i}})}{}

{}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{cw}}{(\exists{{\bar{\beta}}^{\prime}}.\,{\gamma}={\varsigma\,{\bar{\beta}}^{\prime}}\wedge{{i}^{x}}({{\bar{\beta}}})\leadsto{{\bar{\beta}}^{\prime}})}{}

{}={}

{}1+\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{sw}}{(\varsigma)}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{tw}}{({\beta_{i}})}+|{\bar{\beta}}^{\prime}|{}

To ensure a strict decrease, it suffices to show that
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{sw}}{(\varsigma)}>|{\bar{\beta}}^{\prime}|
.
Given that
|{\bar{\beta}}^{\prime}|=|\varsigma|
, and by the definition of
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{sw}}{(\varsigma)}
, this inequality holds.
Therefore, the weight strictly decreases under
S-Inst-Copy
.

Thus the constraint solver terminates.
∎

C.4.
Correctness

Lemma C.16.

Given non-simple
C
constraint. If every match constraint
\mathscr{C}[\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\tau\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}{\bar{\chi}}]=C

satisfies
\mathscr{C}[\tau\mathop{\hbox to0.0pt{$\diagdown$\hss}\diagup\!\!\!{!}\,}]
, then
C
is unsatisfiable.

Proof.

By contradiction, inverting on the canonical derivation of
C
.
∎

Lemma C.17 (Scope preservation).

For all
{C_{1}},{C_{2}}
, if
{C_{1}}\longrightarrow{C_{2}}
, then
\mathsf{fv}({{C_{1}}})\supseteq\mathsf{fv}({{C_{2}}})
.

Proof.

By induction on
{C_{1}}\longrightarrow{C_{2}}
.
∎

Corollary C.18.

For the closed-term-variable constraint
C
,
C
is satisfiable if and only if

C\longrightarrow^{*}\hat{C}
and
\hat{C}
is a solved form equivalent to
C
.

Proof.

We show each direction individually:

1

\implies
.

By transfinite induction on the well-ordering of constraints whose
existence is shown in
Theorem

5.6
.

We have
C
is satisfiable.
By
Theorem

C.14
, we have three cases:

1

C
is solved
.
We have
C\longrightarrow^{*}C
and
C\equiv C
by reflexitivity.
So we are done.

2

C
is stuck
.
Given
C
is a closed-term-variable constraint,
it must be the case that either
C
is
\mathsf{false}

or

\hat{\mathscr{C}}[\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\tau\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}{\bar{\chi}}]
and
\mathscr{C}[\tau\mathop{\hbox to0.0pt{$\diagdown$\hss}\diagup\!\!\!{!}\,}]
.

If
C
is
\mathsf{false}
, this contradicts our assumption that
C
is satisfiable.
Similarly, by
Lemma

C.16
, if
C
is
\hat{\mathscr{C}}[\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{match}}\tau\mathrel{\mathsf{with}}{\bar{\chi}}]
,
then this also contradicts the satisfiability of
C
.

3

C\longrightarrow{C^{\prime}}
.

By
Theorem

5.7
, we have
C\equiv{C^{\prime}}
, thus
{C^{\prime}}
is satisfiable.
Additionally, by
Lemma

C.17
, we have
\mathsf{fv}({{C^{\prime}}})=\emptyset
.
So by induction, we have
{C^{\prime}}\longrightarrow^{*}\hat{C}
and
\hat{C}

is a solved form equivalent to
{C^{\prime}}
.
By transitivity of equivalence, we therefore have
\hat{C}\equiv C
, as
required.

2

\impliedby
.

By induction on the rewriting
C\longrightarrow^{*}\hat{C}
.

1

{\frac{}{\hat{C}\longrightarrow^{*}\hat{C}}\quad\textsc{fraction={{}{-}\to},Right=Zero-Step}}

We have
C=\hat{C}
by inversion. All solved forms are satisfiable, thus

C
is satisfiable.

2

{\frac{C\longrightarrow{C^{\prime}}\hskip 8.19447pt{C^{\prime}}\longrightarrow^{*}\hat{C}}{C\longrightarrow^{*}\hat{C}}\quad\textsc{fraction={{}{-}\to},Right=One-Step}}

By induction, we have
{C^{\prime}}
is satisfiable. By
Theorem

5.7
,

C\equiv{C^{\prime}}
, hence
C
is satisfiable.

∎

Appendix D
Properties of
OmniML

This section states and proves the two central metatheoretic properties of

OmniML
. The first is the
soundness and completeness
of the constraint
generator
\llbracket e:\alpha\rrbracket
with respect to the
OmniML
typing rules. The second
is the existence of
principal types
, which follows as a consequence
of soundness and completeness: every closed well-typed term
e
admits a
most general type.

Throughout this section, we restrict our attention to
closed
terms
. This is because the typing context
\Gamma
can contain bindings to
terms whose type is “guessed”. When we generate constraints for a term

e
under a context
\Gamma
, we encode the type schemes in
\Gamma
as part of the
constraint itself using let-constraints. However, these schemes are
treated as known within the constraint! As a result, we assume terms are
closed from the outside to avoid
\Gamma
leaking any guessed type information.

D.1.
Simple syntax-directed system

As a first step towards proving soundness and completeness of constraint
generation, we first present a variant of the
OmniML
type system for

simple terms
. For this system, the syntax tree completely determines
the derivation tree.

We use the standard technique of removing the
Inst
and
Gen
rules,
and always apply instantiations in
Var
(
Var-SD
) and always
generalize at let-bindings (
Let-SD
). We can show that this system is
sound and complete with respect to the declarative rules.

Theorem D.1 (Soundness of the syntax directed rules).

Given the simple term
e
.
If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}e:\tau
then we also have
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}e:\tau

Proof.

Induction on the given derivation.
∎

Theorem D.2 (Completeness of the syntax directed rules).

Given the simple term
e
.
If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}e:\sigma
, then
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}e:\tau
for any instance
\tau
of
\sigma
.

Proof.

Induction on the given derivation.
∎

Inversion

On a simple syntax-directed derivation
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}e:\tau
, we have the usual inversion
principle:

Lemma D.3 (Simple inversion).

(1)

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}x:\tau
, then
x:\forall{{\bar{\alpha}}}.\,{{\tau^{\prime}}}\in\Gamma
and
\tau={\tau^{\prime}}[{\bar{\alpha}}:={\bar{\tau}}]
.

(2)

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}\lambda x.\,e:\tau
, then
\Gamma,x:{\tau_{1}}\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}e:{\tau_{2}}
and
\tau={\tau_{1}}\to{\tau_{2}}
.

(3)

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}{e_{1}}\;{e_{2}}:\tau
, then
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}{e_{1}}:{\tau^{\prime}}\to\tau
and
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}{e_{2}}:{\tau^{\prime}}
.

(4)

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}():\tau
, then
\tau=1
.

(5)

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x={e_{1}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{e_{2}}:\tau
, then
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}{e_{1}}:{\tau^{\prime}}
,
{\bar{\alpha}}\mathop{\#}\mathsf{fv}({\Gamma})
, and
\Gamma,x:\forall{{\bar{\alpha}}}.\,{{\tau^{\prime}}}\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}{e_{2}}:\tau
.

(6)

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}(e:\exists{\bar{\alpha}}.\,{\tau^{\prime}}):\tau
, then
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}e:{\tau^{\prime}}[{\bar{\alpha}}:={\bar{\tau}}]
and
\tau={\tau^{\prime}}[{\bar{\alpha}}:={\bar{\tau}}]
.

(7)

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}({e_{1}},\ldots,{e_{n}}):\tau
, then
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}{e_{i}}:{\tau_{i}}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
and
\tau=\Pi_{i=1}^{n}{{\tau_{i}}}
.

(8)

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}e.{j}^{n}:\tau
, then
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}e:\Pi_{i=1}^{n}{{\tau_{i}}}
and
\tau={\tau_{j}}
, with
n\geq j
.

(9)

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}[e:\exists{\bar{\alpha}}.\,\forall{{\bar{\beta}}}.\,{{\tau^{\prime}}}]:\tau
, then
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}e:\tau[{\bar{\alpha}}:={\bar{\tau}}]
,
{\bar{\beta}}\mathop{\#}\Gamma
and

\tau=[\forall{{\bar{\beta}}}.\,{{\tau^{\prime}}}][{\bar{\alpha}}:={\bar{\tau}}]
.

(10)

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}\langle e:{\exists{\bar{\alpha}}.\,\sigma}\rangle:\tau
, then
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}e:[\sigma][{\bar{\alpha}}:={\bar{\tau}}]
and
\sigma[{\bar{\alpha}}:={\bar{\tau}}]\leq\tau
.

(11)

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}\square\mathrel{\mathsf{\mathsf{with}}}{{\bar{e}}}:\tau
, then
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}{e_{i}}:{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
.

(12)

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}T.\{\overline{\ell=e}\}:\tau
, then
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}{e_{i}}:{\tau_{i}}
and
{T.\ell}\leq\tau\to{\tau_{i}}
for
1\leq i\leq n
and
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{dom}}(T.\Omega)={\bar{\ell}}
.

(13)

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}\{\overline{\ell=e}\}:\tau
, then
{\bar{\ell}}\mathrel{\blacktriangleright}T
and
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}T.\{\overline{\ell=e}\}:\tau
.

(14)

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}e.{T.\ell}:\tau
, then
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}e:{\tau^{\prime}}
,
{T.\ell}\leq{\tau^{\prime}}\to\tau
.

(15)

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}e.\ell:\tau
, then
\ell\mathrel{\triangleright}T
and
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}e.{T.\ell}:\tau
.

D.2.
Canonicalization of typability

Our system satisfies a similar canonicalization theorem to constraint satisfiability.

Lemma D.4 (Composability of unicity).

(1)

If
{\mathscr{E}_{1}}[\square\mathop{\triangleleft}{\varsigma}\mid{\bar{e}}]
, then
{\mathscr{E}_{2}}[{\mathscr{E}_{1}}][\square\mathop{\triangleleft}{\varsigma}\mid{\bar{e}}]
.

(2)

If
{\mathscr{E}_{1}}[e\mathop{\triangleright}{\varsigma}]
, then
{\mathscr{E}_{2}}[{\mathscr{E}_{1}}][e\mathop{\triangleright}{\varsigma}]
.

Proof.

By induction on
{\mathscr{E}_{2}}
.
∎

Lemma D.5 (Decanonicalization).

If
\Vdash e:\tau
, then
\emptyset\vdash e:\tau
.

Proof.

By induction on the given derivation
\Vdash e:\tau
.
∎

Theorem D.6 (Canonicalization).

If
\vdash e:\sigma
, then
\Vdash e:\tau
for any instance
\tau
of
\sigma
.

Proof.

By induction on the following measure of
e
:

\|e\|\hskip 4.30554pt\mathrel{\triangleq}\hskip 4.30554pt\langle{\#\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{implicit}}{e}},|e|\rangle

where
\langle\ldots\rangle
denotes a lexicographically ordered pair, and

(1)

{\#\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{implicit}}{e}}
is the number of implicit constructs in
e

i.e.
, overloaded tuple projections
e.j
,
implicit non-unique field projections
e.\ell
, implicit non-unique records
\{\overline{\ell=e}\}
, polytype instantiations
\langle e\rangle

and polytype boxing
[e]
.

(2)

the last component
|e|
is a structural measure of terms
i.e.
, a
application
{e_{1}}\;{e_{2}}
is larger than the two terms
{e_{1}},{e_{2}}
.

This measure is analogous to the measure
{\|C\|}
for constraints.
∎

D.3.
Unifiers

A substitution
\vartheta
is an idempotent function from type variables to types.
The (finite) domain of
\vartheta
is the set of type variables such that
\vartheta(\alpha)\neq\alpha
for any
\alpha\in\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{dom}}\vartheta
, while the codomain consists of the free
type variables of its range.
We use the notation
[{\bar{\alpha}}:={\bar{\tau}}]
for the substitution
\vartheta
with
domain
{\bar{\alpha}}
and
\vartheta({\bar{\alpha}})={\bar{\tau}}
.

The constraint induced by a substitution
\vartheta
, written
\exists\vartheta
, is

\exists{{\bar{\beta}}}.\,{\bar{\alpha}}={\bar{\tau}}
where
{\bar{\beta}}=\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{rng}}\vartheta
,
{\bar{\alpha}}=\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{dom}}\vartheta

and
\vartheta({\bar{\alpha}})={\bar{\tau}}
.

Definition D.7 (Unifier).

A substitution
\vartheta
is a unifier of
C
if
\exists\vartheta
entails
C
.
A unifier
\vartheta
of
C
is
most general
when
\exists\vartheta
is equivalent
to
C
.

Lemma D.8 (Simple inversion of unifiers).

•

If
\vartheta
is a unifier of
{{\tau_{1}}}={{\tau_{2}}}
, then
\vartheta({\tau_{1}})=\vartheta({\tau_{2}})
.

•

For simple
{C_{1}},{C_{2}}
, if
\vartheta
is a unifier of
{C_{1}}\wedge{C_{2}}
, then
\vartheta
is a unifier of
{C_{1}}
and
{C_{2}}
.

•

For simple
C
, if
\vartheta
is a unifier of
\exists{\alpha}.\,C
, then
\vartheta[\alpha:=\tau]
is a unifier of
C
for some
\tau
.

•

For simple
C
, if
\vartheta
is a unifier of
\forall{\alpha}.\,C
, then
\vartheta
is a unifier of
C
.

Proof.

Follows by simple inversion.
∎

Lemma D.9.

If
\vartheta
unifies
\exists{\alpha}.\,C
, then there exists a unifier
{\vartheta^{\prime}}
that extends
\vartheta
with
\alpha
,
where
{\vartheta^{\prime}}
is most general unifier of
\exists\vartheta\wedge C
.

Then
\lambda{\alpha}.\,{C}
is equivalent to
\lambda{\alpha}.\,{\sigma}\leq\alpha
under
\vartheta
, where
\sigma=\forall{{\bar{\beta}}}.\,{{\vartheta^{\prime}}}(\alpha)
and

{\bar{\beta}}=\mathsf{fv}({{\vartheta^{\prime}}(\alpha)})\setminus\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{rng}}\vartheta
. We write this equivalent constraint abstraction as
\llbracket\lambda{\alpha}.\,{C}\rrbracket_{\vartheta}
.

Proof.

See
29
.
∎

Lemma D.10 (Let inversion of unifiers).

For simple
{C_{1}},{C_{2}}
.
If
\vartheta
unifies
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x=\lambda{\alpha}.\,{{C_{1}}}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{2}}
, then

\vartheta
unifies
\exists{\alpha}.\,{C_{1}}
and

\vartheta
unifies
\mathopen{}\mathrel{\mathsf{let}}x=\llbracket\lambda{\alpha}.\,{{C_{1}}}\rrbracket_{\vartheta}\mathrel{\mathsf{in}}{C_{2}}

Proof.

Follows from
Lemma

D.9
and simple inversion.
∎

Lemma D.11.

For two substitutions
\vartheta
,
{\vartheta^{\prime}}
. If
\exists\vartheta\vDash\exists{\vartheta^{\prime}}
, there exists

{\vartheta^{\prime\prime}}
such that
\vartheta={\vartheta^{\prime\prime}}\circ{\vartheta^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Standard result, follows from definition of
\exists\vartheta
.
∎

D.4.
Soundness and completeness of constraint generation

Lemma D.12.

For any term context
\mathscr{E}
, term
e
,
\llbracket\mathscr{E}[\square:\tau]:{\tau^{\prime}}\rrbracket[\llbracket e:\tau\rrbracket]=\llbracket\mathscr{E}[e]:{\tau^{\prime}}\rrbracket
.

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
\mathscr{E}
.
∎

Lemma D.13.

For any term
e
,
\lfloor\llbracket e:\tau\rrbracket\rfloor=\llbracket\lfloor e\rfloor:\tau\rrbracket
.

Proof.

By induction on
e
.
∎

Lemma D.14 (Simple soundness and completeness).

For simple terms
e
.

\vartheta(\Gamma)\vdash_{\mathsf{simple}}^{\mathsf{sd}}e:\vartheta(\tau)
if and only if
\vartheta
is a unifier of
\llbracket\Gamma\vdash e:\tau\rrbracket
.

Proof.

By induction on
e\mathrel{\mathsf{simple}}\mathclose{}
.
∎

\vdash\,

\llbracket\mathscr{E}[\square\mathrel{\mathsf{\mathsf{with}}}{(e:{\mathfrak{g}})}]:\alpha\rrbracket

By definition

\mathscr{C}[{\alpha_{1}}\mathop{!}\nu{\bar{\gamma}}.\,\Pi_{i=1}^{n}{\bar{\gamma}}]

Premise

\mathsf{shape}({\mathfrak{g}})

=\,

\nu{\bar{\gamma}}.\,\Pi_{i=1}^{n}{\bar{\gamma}}

\implies
E

\mathscr{E}[e\mathop{\triangleright}{\nu{\bar{\gamma}}.\,\Pi_{i=1}^{n}{\bar{\gamma}}}]

Above

\Vdash

\mathscr{E}[e.j]:\vartheta(\alpha)

By
Can-Proj-I

2

[e]
,
\langle e\rangle
,
\{\overline{\ell=e}\}
,
e.\ell
.

Similar arguments.

∎

D.5.
Principal types

See
4.12

Proof.

Let
e
be an arbitrary closed well-typed term; that is, there exists a type

\tau
such that
\vdash e:\tau
.
By
Theorem

D.15
, the constraint
\llbracket e:\alpha\rrbracket
is satisfiable
(specifically under the unifier
{\alpha}={\tau}
). By
Corollary

C.18
, there
exists a solved constraint
\hat{C}
such that
\hat{C}\equiv\llbracket e:\alpha\rrbracket
.
From
\hat{C}
, we extract a unifier
\vartheta
. Since
\hat{C}\equiv\exists\vartheta
,
it follows that
\vartheta
is
most general
.

We claim that
\vartheta(\alpha)
is the principal type of
e
. This amounts to showing:

(1)

\vdash e:\vartheta(\alpha)

(2)

For any other typing
\vdash e:{\tau^{\prime}}
, then
{\tau^{\prime}}=\theta(\vartheta(\alpha))
for some
\theta
.

Since
\vartheta
is a unifier of
\llbracket e:\alpha\rrbracket
, it follows immediately from

Theorem

D.15
that
\vdash e:\vartheta(\alpha)
, proving
1
.
For
2
, suppose
\vdash e:{\tau^{\prime}}
for some
{\tau^{\prime}}
. Then by

Theorem

D.15
again, there exists a unifier
{\vartheta^{\prime}}
of
\llbracket e:\alpha\rrbracket

such that
{\vartheta^{\prime}}(\alpha)={\tau^{\prime}}
. Since
\vartheta
is most general, we have
\exists{\vartheta^{\prime}}\vDash\exists\vartheta
,
and by
Lemma

D.11
, this implies the existence of a substitution
{\vartheta^{\prime\prime}}
such that

{\vartheta^{\prime}}={\vartheta^{\prime\prime}}\circ\vartheta
.
Hence,
{\tau^{\prime}}={\vartheta^{\prime}}(\alpha)={\vartheta^{\prime\prime}}(\vartheta(\alpha))
, witnessing that
{\tau^{\prime}}
is an instance of
\vartheta(\alpha)
, as
required
2
.
∎
